Asymptotic analysis of boundary layers for Stokes systems in periodic homogenization
Moustapha Agne 1

Abstract
. We investigate the asymptotics of boundary layers in periodic homogenization. The analysis is focused on a Stokes system with periodic coefficients and periodic Dirichlet data posed in the half-space { y ∈ ℝ d : y ⋅ n − s > 0 } \{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y\cdot n-s>0\} . In particular, we establish the convergence of the velocity as y ⋅ n → ∞ y\cdot n\rightarrow\infty . We obtain this convergence for arbitrary normals n ∈ 𝕊 d − 1 n\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1} ​. Moreover, we build an asymptotic expansion of Poisson’s kernel for the periodically oscillating Stokes operator in the half-space. The presence of the pressure and the incompressibility condition impose certain innovations. In particular, we provide a framework for the analysis of the boundary layers in homogenization that relies only on physical space techniques and not on techniques that rely on the quasiperiodic structure of the problem.



1.
Introduction

In this paper we study the asymptotics of
u^{s}=u^{s}(y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
where
(u^{s},p^{s})
is a solution to the Stokes system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(y)\nabla u^{s}+\nabla p^{s}&=&0&\mbox{ in }\{y\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{s}&=&0,\\
u^{s}&=&g(y)&\mbox{ on }\{y\cdot n-s=0\}\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

set in the half-space
\{y\cdot n-s>0\}
and with periodically oscillating coefficients
A=A(y)
and boundary data
g=g(y)
.

Homogenization can be defined as a process of averaging some micro-scale features to obtain proper-

ties at a level that represent the overall behavior of an object of study. It is used throughout the sciences and in particular in physics and mechanics with the study of composite media. In mathematics and, in particular, in the theory of Partial Differential Equations (PDE) the topic of homogenization comes in the form of equations as in (
1.2
). In these equations or PDE the parameter
\varepsilon
usually represents a small number accounting for a micro-scale.

1.1.
Motivations

The solution to problem (
1.1
) is a half-space boundary layer corrector. Indeed, it is used for refining asymptotic expansions in homogenization theory for problems involving the presence of a boundary. Let us describe briefly how this application works. A well-known method of analyzing the solution
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
to the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}&=&f&\mbox{ in }\{x\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}&=&h,&\\
u^{\varepsilon}&=&g&\mbox{ on }\{x\cdot n-s=0\}\end{array}\right.

(1.2)

with highly oscillating coefficients but non-oscillating boundary data is by introducing a multi-scale expansion
of the form

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=u^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+...

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=p^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon p^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+...

where
u^{k}=u^{k}(x,y),p^{k}=p^{k}(x,y)
,
k=0,1
, are periodic in the variable
y
and the pair
(u^{0},p^{0})
satisfies the so-called homogenized problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A^{0}\nabla u^{0}+\nabla p^{0}&=&f&\mbox{ in }\{x\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{0}&=&h,&\\
u^{0}&=&g&\mbox{ on }\{x\cdot n-s=0\}.\end{array}\right.

See subsection
2.1
for more details on the multi-scale expansion.

Such an Ansatz provides a satisfactory approximation of the solution
u^{\varepsilon}
in the interior of the domain as it captures the periodic micro-structure of the domain. Nonetheless, in general, the boundary breaks the periodic structure : the periodicity of the term
u^{1}=u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)
in the variable
x/\varepsilon
is incompatible with the Dirichlet conditions

\displaystyle u^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+...=g\quad\mbox{and}\quad u^{0}=g\quad\mbox{ on }\{x\cdot n-s=0\}.

Hence we add in the Ansatz a boundary layer term
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=u^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}+...,

that acts as to compensate for this incompatibility. In fact, the boundary layer term
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}
is chosen to satisfy
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}+u^{1}=0
on the boundary
\{x\cdot n-s=0\}
. In other words the boundary layer corrector is introduced to account for the effects of the boundary on the Ansatz at order
\varepsilon
. More precisely, the quantity
u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)
is taken to equal
\chi(x/\varepsilon)\cdot\nabla u^{0}(x)
which is highly oscillating and the boundary layer term
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}
satifies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p_{bl}^{\varepsilon}&=&0&\mbox{ in }\{x\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}&=&0,&\\
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}&=&-\chi(x/\varepsilon)\cdot\nabla u^{0}(x)&\mbox{ on }\{x\cdot n-s=0\}.\end{array}\right.

As a consequence, the addition of the boundary layer term to the expansion leads to refined estimates on the error
u^{\varepsilon}-u^{0}
as shown for instance in
[
3
,
12
]
for elliptic equations and in
[
13
]
for Stokes in the slightly different context of oscillating boundaries.

The study in the present article focuses on the geometry of the half-space. It is a simple case geometry but it retains the essential difficulties and it is also a basic element in the case of most general smooth curved domain where the half-space case is used as a building block (see
[
5
,
11
]
and
Figure

1
).

Among the interests of problem (
1.2
) is that it models some physical and mechanical objects with a composite micro-structure. It is also used in the modeling of flows in a domain with a rough boundary as in
[
13
,
20
]
. Indeed, let us outline the connection between the flow over a rough boundary and system (
1.2
). Via a change of variables one flattens the boundary which transfers the oscillations from the boundary into the coefficients of the Stokes operator. Moreover, the system (
1.2
) is related to the first-order expansion of eigenvalue problems for the Stokes operator with oscillating coefficients as in the elliptic case (
[
21
,
25
,
33
]
).

1.2.
State of the art

There is an important body of works dealing with the topic of boundary layers in homogenization and related topics such as the oscillating boundary problem as well as the oscillating data problem (
[
2
,
3
,
6
,
11
,
13
,
24
,
28
,
29
,
32
]
). Let us examine this literature with the angle of the equations and geometry.

1.2.1.
A variety of equations

The oscillating boundary value problems have drawn the attention of several authors. The common feature is the fact that the boundary data is oscillating. In particular, elliptic systems with an oscillating boundary condition were extensively investigated by many authors
[
2
,
3
,
9
,
11
,
12
,
20
,
24
,
28
,
32
]
. Moreover, Dirichlet
[
2
,
5
]
and Neumann boundary conditions were studied in
[
29
]
. Fully nonlinear equations with oscillating Dirichlet boundary conditions were also studied in
[
9
,
6
]
. Besides, the Stokes system and the stationary Navier-Stokes equations were examined in the context of rough boundaries
[
13
,
17
]
.

1.2.2.
The geometry of the domain

The boundary of the domain plays an important role in many PDE problems. This is particularly the case when the problem presents oscillations phenomena near the boundary such as the oscillating boundary data problems.
A fundamental feature of the problem that is present in the elliptic as well as in the Stokes case is the way the boundary interacts with the periodic structure. Locally, this interaction is the way the tangent hyperplane intersects the lattice. The number of hyperplanes needed to determine or approximate the domain may be one or an arbitrary large number.

(1)
Half-space :
This corresponds to the case where the domain is determined by a unique hyperplane. This hyperplane being defined by its unit normal vector
n\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
one can distinguish two situations:
n
is rational and
n
is irrational. When
n
is rational i.e.
n\in\mathbb{R}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
one is in fact in a periodic setting. Indeed, in this case one has
n=\lambda v,\lambda\in\mathbb{R},v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which is a definite intersection of the line
\{\lambda n,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}
with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Then up to a normalization, considering
|v|
as the reference length, and by the periodicity of
A
and
g
we get
A(y+hv)=A(y),\ g(y+hv)=g(v)
, for all
h\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is the periodicity in the normal direction which, by the orthogonality of the periodic structure, yields the periodicity in the tangential direction. In the case
n
is irrational the line
\{\lambda n,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}
has no intersection with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Yet there are points of the lattice that are arbitrarily close to the line. However, there are irrational
n
for which the distance
\dist(h,\{\lambda n,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}),h\in\mathbb{Z}^{d},
is, in some sense, bounded below by a positive number. More precisely, such irrational direction
n
satisfy a condition of the type : there exist
A(n),\tau>0
such that

\displaystyle|P_{n^{\perp}}(h)|\geq\frac{A(n)}{|h|^{d+\tau}},\quad\mbox{ for all }h\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\char 92\relax\{0\},

(1.3)

where
P_{n^{\perp}}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the hyperplane
n^{\perp}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y\cdot n=s\}.

This so-called Diophantine condition is a non-resonance condition. It implies a quantitative ergodic property of the half-space boundary layer which yields a convergence to a boundary layer tail faster than any polynomial rate of convergence (see
[
12
,
13
]
).

The case of general irrational normals
n
for elliptic systems is treated in the article
[
24
]
. It is shown in that article that for an arbitrary
n\notin\mathbb{R}\mathbb{Z}^{d}
the convergence of the boundary layer can be arbitrarily slow.

Studies concerning the half-space domain are found in
[
5
,
6
,
11
]
. Some authors take the polygon to be the intersection of half-spaces and thus consider the half-space problem as a part in their analysis of equations posed in a polygonal domain. Others approximate the smooth and uniformly convex domain by a polygon and thus approximate the boundary by a sequence of hyperplanes (see
Figure

1
).

(2)
Polygons :
A polygon can be determined by a finite sequence of hyperplanes. Polygons make a part of the family of bounded domains which are extensively considered in the literature. The article
[
3
]
treats the problem in a rectangular domain which is a special case of a polygon. A study considering more general polygon is found in
[
2
,
12
]
.
For polygons, the non-smoothness at the vertices is a source of difficulty.

(3)
Smooth domain :
For curved domains
\Omega
we require the domain to have a certain regularity e.g. to be smooth and uniformly convex (
[
29
,
5
,
11
]
). The assumption of smoothness of the boundary implies that the unit normal
n(x),\ x\in\partial\Omega,
is smooth in
x
, which plays a part in the regularity of the homogenized boundary data in
[
5
]
. This is important because it allows applications of boundary regularity estimates. The uniform convexity allows to get a control on the constant
A(n)
in the Diophantine condition (
1.3
) for instance in
[
5
]
we have that

A^{-1}(n(x)),\,x\in\partial\Omega
, as a function of
x\in\partial\Omega
, belongs to the weak Lebesgue space

L^{d-1,\infty}(\partial\Omega)
.

Figure 1.
Approximating a smooth domain by a set of hyperplanes.

Let us also mention the articles
[
5
,
11
,
28
,
32
]
that deal with the homogenization of an elliptic system in bounded smooth domains. In these articles, the authors use a technique by which the boundary is broken into pieces and each piece is approximated by a hyperplane, thus, making the half-space problem an entire step in their study. The article
[
5
]

treats important issues such as the thickness of the boundary layer. Knowing this thickness allows to estimate how far from the boundary one can start to use correctly the interior Ansatz without the boundary layer corrector.

One of the goals of the study of the asymptotics of the boundary layer in the half-space is to find the term that must be added to the interior Ansatz so that it remains valid next to the boundary. Moreover, the question of the convergence far from the boundary is related to the problem of homogenization of the oscillating boundary data. In fact, the homogenized boundary data is constructed from the boundary layer tail as shown in
[
29
]
. In the present article, one intends to adapt the methods of
[
24
]
to the Stokes system.

1.3.
Difficulties specific to the Stokes system

The questions of existence and uniqueness of a solution to system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(y)\nabla u^{s}&=&0&\mbox{ in }\{y\cdot n>s\},\\
u^{s}&=&g&\mbox{ on }\{y\cdot n=s\}.\end{array}\right.

have been handled in many articles by using the quasiperiodicity of the data in the hyperplane
\{y\cdot n=s\}
(see
[
11
,
12
,
29
]
),

\displaystyle g(M(z^{\prime},z_{d}))=V_{0}(Nz^{\prime},z_{d}),\quad M^{T}A(M(z^{\prime},z_{d}))M=\mathcal{B}(Nz^{\prime},z_{d}),\quad

where
V_{0}=V_{0}(\theta,t),\,\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}(\theta,t)
are periodic in
\theta
,
and by turning the problem into one posed in the cylinder
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\vskip 7.22743pt-\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot\mathcal{B}(\theta,t)\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)V&=&0,&(\theta,t)\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
V&=&V_{0}(\theta,0),&\theta\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{array}\right.

There is a cost in the move from the half-space to the higher dimension cylinder which is the loss of the ellipticity of the differential operator. Indeed, through this move we get a degenerate gradient. This degeneracy is handled by a regularization method. For instance, the existence of a solution is obtained by some authors through this regularization argument. It consists in using the compactness of the sequence
(V_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
of solutions e.g. to elliptic systems

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\vskip 7.22743pt-\delta\Delta_{\theta}V_{\delta}-\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot\mathcal{B}(\theta,t)\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)V_{\delta}&=&0,&(\theta,t)\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
V_{\delta}&=&V_{0}(\theta,0),&\theta\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{array}\right.

The article
[
13
]
follows the same lines as for the well-posedness issue yet it studies a Stokes system. Nonetheless the Stokes problem treated therein, posed in a half-space, is divergence-free and has a homogeneous boundary data

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(y)\nabla u+\nabla p&=&f&\mbox{ in }\{y\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u&=&0,&\\
u&=&0&\mbox{ on }\{y\cdot n-s=0\}.\end{array}\right.

Therefore the operation that turns this problem posed in a half-space into one posed in the cylinder preserve the solenoidal aspect of the problem and the homogenous Dirichlet condition

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\vskip 7.22743pt-\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot\mathcal{B}(\theta,t)\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)V+\;M\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)R&=&G,&(\theta,t)\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\vskip 7.22743pt\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot\ M^{T}V&=&0,\\
V&=&0,&\theta\mbox{ in }\mathbb{T}^{d}.\end{array}\right.

(1.4)

In this case one successfully carries out energy estimates that lead to uniqueness for instance. In contrast, in the case of a Stokes problem with a non-homogenous boundary condition such as g in (
1.1
), lifting the boundary data leads to technical difficulties. Indeed, there is no obvious divergence-free lifting of the boundary data.
The switching to the higher dimension cylinder
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
is also used in the article
[
5
]
for comparing two boundary layers
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
associated respectively to normal vectors
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. The authors study the difference
W=V_{1}-V_{2}
where
V_{1},V_{2}
are defined on the same domain
\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
and are respective representations of
u_{1},u_{2}.

Moreover, there are formulae given in
[
29
]
(adapted in the present orientation of the normal
n
) :
when a solution
u^{s}
to problem (
1.1
)
is in the form

\displaystyle u^{s}(y)

\displaystyle=V(y-(y\cdot n-s)n,y\cdot n-s),

(1.5)

then
V=V(\theta,t)
has satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}\vskip 7.22743pt-\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot\mathcal{B}(\theta,t)\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)V+\;M\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)R&=&0,&(\theta,t)\mbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
\vskip 7.22743pt\left(\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}N^{T}\nabla_{\theta}\\
\partial_{t}\end{array}\!\!\!\right)\cdot M^{T}V&=&0,\\
V&=&g(\theta),&\theta\mbox{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{array}\right.

(1.6)

Conversely, the identity (
1.5
) yields

\displaystyle V(\theta,t)

\displaystyle=u^{\theta\cdot n}(\theta+tn),\quad(\theta,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(1.7)

By these formulae one can readily switch from the system (
1.6
)
to the problem (
1.1
) set in the physical space.
In particular, one can perform computations directly on
u^{s}
to derive estimates on
V
.

1.4.
Statement of the main Result

In this article we aim at showing the following :

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a unique solution
u^{s}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{n}(s))
to problem
(
1.1
)
such that

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{H}_{n}(s)}\;(y\cdot n-s)|\nabla u^{s}(y)|<\infty.

(1.8)

Moreover, as
y\cdot n\rightarrow\infty
this solution converges to a constant vector fields
U^{s,\infty}(n)

(
see formula

(
6.3
)
in
Section
6
)
and this limit does not depend on
s
when
n
is irrational.

1.5.
Strategy of the proof

This article aims at establishing the proof of Theorem
1.1
. It focuses on the convergence to a boundary layer tail. Besides, a proof of the uniqueness of the solution
u^{s}
under hypothesis (
1.8
) is presented in the Appendix. Our method uses an integral representation of the solution to problem (
1.1
). To put it in more details, we use a representation via the Poisson kernel associated to the Stokes operator and the domain
\{y\cdot n>0\}
. In fact, for most of the paper we will consider the case
s=0
in problem (
1.1
). One of the reasons for such a choice is that one can make re-scalings without changing the domain. Most of the results and estimates are uniform in
s
and continue to hold in the case of an arbitrary
s\in\mathbb{R}
up to a translation in the formulae. A pivotal point of our method is obtaining an asymptotic expansion of the Poisson kernel which subsequently leads to the asymptotic behavior of the boundary layer. We use uniform estimates to control this expansion.
These are important tools for the study of problems involving oscillating coefficients. In this context they correspond to estimates of
u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon}
uniformly in
\varepsilon
. For instance, in the elliptic case, Avellaneda and Lin established uniform Lipschitz estimates. In the case of a Stokes system, the article
[
16
]
presents a uniform Lipschitz estimate for the velocity
u^{\varepsilon}
and a uniform oscillation estimate for the pressure, see also
[
14
,
15
,
17
]
.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

This article is organized as follows. It starts with an introduction that gives an overview of the question. In Section
2
we recall the construction of the interior correctors and give an Ansatz for the heterogeneous quantities
(u^{\varepsilon},\,p^{\varepsilon})
satisfying a Stokes system. Estimates on Green’s functions are also mentioned in this section. Then we move on to Section
3
, where a homogenization result is established for a Stokes system posed in the half-space. This homogenization result is applied in Section
4
, where we study the homogenization of the Green function. In Section
5
we get asymptotic expansions of Green’s functions and of Poisson’s kernel.
Section
6
is where we give the proof of the convergence towards a boundary layer tail.

1.7.
Notation and main assumptions

In this paper we use the small case letter
d
to denote the dimension of the domain and we shall consider
d\geq 3
unless stated otherwise. For
n\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we use
\mathbb{H}_{n}(s)
to denote the set
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y\cdot n-s>0\}
. Further, a disk in
\mathbb{H}_{n}(s)
is denoted by
D_{r}(y)=B_{r}(y)\cap\mathbb{H}_{n}(s)
where
B_{r}(y)
is the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with center
y
and radius
r
and
\Gamma_{r}(y):=B_{r}(y)\cap\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}(s)
. We occasionally use
Y
to denote the fundamental cell :
Y=\mathbb{T}^{d}\simeq(0,1]^{d}.

The case
s=0
: the half-space
\mathbb{H}_{n}(0)
is simply written
\mathbb{H}_{n}
and in this case the solutions
(u^{s},p^{s})
to (
1.1
) are simply denoted by
(u,p)
. Our analysis of problem (
1.1
) will mostly be focused on the case
s=0
and occasionally when referring to problem (
1.1
) we may implicitely consider the case
s=0
. The vector
e_{j}
is the
j^{th}
element of the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We frequently use
M
to denote a rotation sending
e_{d}=(0,...,0,1)
to
n
and we shall employ
N
to denote the matrix made of the
d-1
first columns of the matrix
M
.
The oscillation of a function
f
over a domain
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
will be denoted

\underset{D}{\textup{osc}}f
and it is defined by

\osc_{D}f:=\sup_{w,z\in D}|f(w)-f(z)|.

For
A(y)=(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)),
we set
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}:=-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla\cdot
to be a divergence form differential operator that reads

(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})^{i}=-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}\right).

We define the matrix
A^{*}
to be the adjoint matrix of
A
i.e.
(A^{*})^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}=A^{\beta\alpha}_{ji}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,...,d\}
and for all
i,j\in\{1,...,d\}
and the adjoint operator is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\varepsilon}:=-\nabla\cdot A^{*}(x/\varepsilon)\nabla
. We shall frequently use the Hölder semi-norm
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}(Y)}
,
\eta>0
, of
A
which we write
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
in short.
The term heterogeneous refers to quantities
u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon},G^{\varepsilon},P^{\varepsilon}...
that depend on
\varepsilon
.

We assume that the matrix of coefficients is real and satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

ellipticity: there exists
\mu>0,
such that

\displaystyle\mu|w|^{2}\leq A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)w^{\alpha}_{i}w^{\beta}_{j}\leq\frac{1}{\mu}|w|^{2}\quad\mbox{ for all }w\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\mbox{ and for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d};

(1.9)

(2)

periodicity:

\displaystyle A(y+h)=A(y)\quad\mbox{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mbox{ and for all }h\in\mathbb{Z}^{d};

(1.10)

(3)

regularity:

\mbox{ A belongs to }C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).\\

(1.11)

We also assume that the boundary data
g
satisfies

(1)

periodicity : the boundary data is assumed to be periodic, i.e.

\displaystyle g(y+h)=g(y)\quad\mbox{ for all }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mbox{ and for all }h\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

(1.12)

(2)

regularity :

\mbox{the boundary data g belongs to }C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(1.13)

2.
Preliminaries

This section is, for the most part, dedicated to introducing some preparatory elements and tools that will be used repeatedly. These preliminaries include among others the construction of an Ansatz for the Stokes equations. It is also the occasion to present some useful estimates. Let us start with a result that gives a control of the pressure field by the derivative of the velocity.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
8
]
, Lemma 3.4)
.

Let
r>0
. For a pair
(u^{s},p^{s})
satisfying the two first equations of system
(
1.1
)
we have

\displaystyle\|p^{s}-(p^{s})_{D_{r}(0)}\|_{L^{2}(D_{r}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla u^{s}\|_{L^{2}(D_{r}(0))},

(2.1)

where
C
depends
d
and
\mu
.

This Lemma has a generalization in the following sense.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
r>0
. For
q\in(1,\infty)
and for a pair
(u^{s},p^{s})
satisfying the first two equations of system
(
1.1
)
we have

\|p^{s}-(p^{s})_{D_{r}(0)}\|_{L^{q}(D_{r}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla u^{s}\|_{L^{q}(D_{r}(0))},

where
C
depends
d
and
\mu
.

A proof of Lemma
2.2
is given in Appendix
A
.

We will repeatedly use asymptotic expansions of heterogeneous quantities. These are multi-scale expansions that are constructed with the aid of the following correctors

2.1.
Interior correctors

2.1.1.
First-order correctors.

Let
\chi^{\beta}_{j}=(\chi^{\beta}_{kj})_{k}
and
\pi^{\beta}_{j}
be 1-periodic functions that satisfy the following system of equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}-\nabla\cdot A(y)\>\nabla(\chi^{\beta}_{j}+P^{\beta}_{j})+\nabla\pi^{\beta}_{j}&=&0\;\quad\mbox{ in }Y,\\
\nabla\cdot\chi^{\beta}_{j}&=&0\;\quad\mbox{ in }Y,\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\chi^{\beta}_{j}(y)dy&=&0.\end{array}\right.

(2.2)

where
P^{\beta}_{j}
is the function defined by
P^{\beta}_{j}(y)=y_{j}e_{\beta}
. These are the first-order interior correctors for the Stokes system.

2.1.2.
Second-order correctors

Let
\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{j}=(\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{kj})_{k}
and
Q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}
be 1-periodic functions satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\partial_{y_{\gamma}}A^{\gamma\delta}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\delta}}\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{kj}+\partial_{y_{i}}Q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}&=&\,B^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\!-\!\overline{B^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}}-\delta_{i\alpha}\,\pi^{\beta}_{j}(y)&\mbox{ in }Y,\\
\nabla_{y}\cdot\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)&=&-\chi^{\beta}_{\alpha j}(y)&\mbox{ in }Y,\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)dy&=&0,\end{array}\right.\\

(2.3)

where

\displaystyle B^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)

\displaystyle:=A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\!+\!\partial_{y_{\gamma}}[A^{\gamma\alpha}_{ik}(y)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)]\!+\!A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)

\displaystyle\mbox{and }\delta_{i\alpha}

\displaystyle\mbox{ is the Kronecker delta}.

2.1.3.
Estimates on the correctors

Many quantities that we are going to study are built from the above correctors. We shall particularly need to control these quantities and for this reason we would like the correctors to be bounded.

Lemma 2.3
.

Seeing that the coefficients
A
satisfy assumption
(
1.11
)
we have the following estimates on the above correctors :

\displaystyle\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq C,\quad\|\pi\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}\leq C,

\displaystyle\|\Gamma\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq C,\quad\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}\leq C.

The constant
C
in these estimates depends on
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
. Furthermore, the derivatives of these functions are similarly bounded:

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\lambda}_{y}\,\chi\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq C,\quad\|\partial^{\lambda}_{y}\,\pi\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}\leq C,

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\lambda}_{y}\,\Gamma\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}

\displaystyle\leq C,\quad\|\partial^{\lambda}_{y}\,Q\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}\leq C,

for any multi-index
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
.

2.2.
Asymptotic expansion

With these correctors one builds an interior multi-scale expansion of the solution
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
to problem (
1.2
) :

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=u^{0}(x)+\varepsilon u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{2}u^{2}(x,x/\varepsilon)+...

(2.4)

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=p^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)+\varepsilon p^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)+...

(2.5)

where
(u^{0},p^{0})
satisfies the homogenized problem associated to (
1.2
)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A^{0}\nabla u^{0}+\nabla p^{0}&=&f&\mbox{ in }\{x\cdot n-s>0\},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{0}&=&h,&\\
u^{0}&=&g&\mbox{ on }\{x\cdot n-s=0\}\end{array}\right.

and where

\displaystyle(A^{0})_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}:=\frac{1}{|Y|}\int_{Y}[A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)+A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)]dy,

and

\displaystyle u^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)=\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(x)+\tilde{u}_{k}^{1}(x),\quad u^{2}(x,x/\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}

(2.6)

\displaystyle p^{0}(x,x/\varepsilon)=\tilde{p}^{0}(x)+\pi^{\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(x),\quad p^{1}(x,x/\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle Q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}

(2.7)

with

\tilde{u}_{k}^{1}
and
\tilde{p}^{0}
being functions independent of
y

and
(\chi^{\beta}_{j},\pi^{\beta}_{j})
,
(\Gamma^{\alpha\beta}_{j},Q^{\alpha\beta}_{j})
are the correctors given in (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) respectively.

Remark 2.4
.

We occasionally use the homogenized equation written into coordinates

\displaystyle-(A^{0})_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)+\partial_{x_{i}}p^{0}(x)=

\displaystyle f^{i}(x).

(2.8)

We moreover define the correctors
(\chi^{*},\pi^{*})
and
(\Gamma^{*},Q^{*})
associated with the adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1}
in a similar way. These correctors have the above properties of
(\chi,\pi)
and
(\Gamma,Q)
respectively. In particular, they satisfy the estimates in Lemma
2.3
.

2.3.
Some uniform regularity estimates

In our study, we take advantage of the following results to derive estimates uniform in
\varepsilon
for the heterogeneous Green function.

First, we present a result of a uniform Hölder regularity estimate.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
l>0
. Suppose the pair
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\>\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}&=&f&\mbox{in }D_{1}(0),\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}&=&0&\mbox{in }D_{1}(0),\\
u^{\varepsilon}&=&g&\mbox{on }\Gamma_{1}(0),\end{array}\right.

(2.9)

with

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(D_{1}(0))}<\infty,\qquad\|f\|_{L^{d+l}(D_{1}(0))}<\infty,\qquad[g]_{C^{0,1}(\Gamma_{1}(0))}<\infty.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
d,\mu,\|A\|_{C^{0,\eta}}
and
l,
such that for
\lambda=1-d/(d+l)
,

\displaystyle[u^{\varepsilon}]_{C^{0,\lambda}(D_{1/2}(0))}\leq C\left(\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(D_{1}(0))}+\|f\|_{L^{d+l}(D_{1}(0))}+[g]_{C^{0,1}(\Gamma_{1}(0))}\right).

Besides the Hölder estimate there is a uniform Lipschitz estimate given by the following Theorem.

Theorem 2.6
.

(
[
16
]
, Theorem 3.1.)
.
Suppose that the matrix
A
of the coefficients satisfies the ellipticity condition
(
1.9
)
and periodicity
(
1.10
)
and
A\in C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1))
for some
\eta\in(0,1]
. For
\varepsilon>0,
let
(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon})
be a weak solution of

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\>\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}&=&f+\nabla\cdot F,&x\in D(0,1),\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}&=&h,&x\in D(0,1),\\
u^{\varepsilon}&=&g,&x\in\Gamma(0,1),\end{array}\right.

(2.10)

where
f\in L^{d+l}(D(0,1)),
for some
l>0
,
F\in C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1)),\;h\in C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1))
and
g\in C^{1,\eta}(\Gamma(0,1))
, for some
\eta\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(D(0,1/2))}+\ \underset{D(0,1/2)}{\textup{osc}}[p^{\varepsilon}]\leq C\left\{\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(D(0,1))}\right.

\displaystyle+\|f\|_{L^{d+l}(D(0,1))}+[F]_{C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1))}

(2.11)

\displaystyle+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(D(0,1))}+[h]_{C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1))}

\displaystyle+\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(0,1))}

\displaystyle+\|\nabla_{\!\!\textup{ tan}}\,g\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(0,1))}+\left.[\nabla_{\!\!\textup{ tan}}\;g]_{C^{0,\eta}(\Gamma(0,1))}\right\}

where
C
depends on
d,l,\eta,\mu
and
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}(D(0,1))}
but
C
is uniform in
\varepsilon
.

Remark 2.7
.

The original estimate in
[
16
]
, Theorem 3.1, is given in a rescaled version. Using the uniformity of
C
in
\varepsilon
one can go from this version to the rescaled one and conversely. By the inequality

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{D(0,1)}|u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(D(0,1))}

the original estimate implies estimate (
2.11
) because in the original estimate we have the term
\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{D(0,1)}|u^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}
in the place of
\|u^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(D(0,1))}
in estimate (
2.11
).

2.3.1.
Green’s function and Poisson’s kernel for the Stokes system

Our strategy is based on a representation formula via the Poisson kernel.
Because of this representation the study of the behaviour of
u
far away from the boundary is transferred to that of Poisson’s kernel. Yet, the Poisson kernel is constructed from Green’s functions. Therefore these functions are going to be frequently used in our study.

Green’s functions for Stokes system (
1.1
) in the half-space
{\mathbb{H}}_{n}
can be regarded as a family
(G,\Pi)=(G_{j},\Pi_{j})_{j=1,...,d}
where for each
j=1,...,d,
and for
y\neq\tilde{y}
,
G_{j}(y,\tilde{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a vector and
\Pi_{j}(y,\tilde{y})\in\mathbb{R}
is a scalar, satisfying

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla_{y}\cdot A(y)\>\nabla_{y}G_{j}(\cdot,\tilde{y})+\nabla_{y}\Pi_{j}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&\delta(\cdot-\tilde{y})e_{j}&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
\nabla_{y}\cdot G_{j}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0,&\\
G_{j}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}\\
\end{array}\right.

where
\delta
is the Dirac distribution.

The
i^{th}
component of vector
G_{j}(y,\tilde{y})
is denoted
G_{ij}(y,\tilde{y})
.

Occasionally we write the above system of equations in the following more condensed form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla_{y}\cdot A(y)\>\nabla_{y}G(\cdot,\tilde{y})+\nabla_{y}\Pi(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&\delta(\cdot-\tilde{y})I&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
\nabla_{y}\cdot G(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0,&\\
G(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}.\end{array}\right.

(2.12)

Green’s functions
(G^{*},\Pi^{*})=(G_{j}^{*},\Pi_{j}^{*})_{j=1,...,d}
associated to the adjoint operator and to the half-space
\mathbb{H}_{n}
are defined to be the solution to problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\cdot A^{*}(\tilde{y})\>\nabla_{\tilde{y}}G^{*}(\cdot,y)+\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\Pi^{*}(\cdot,y)&=&\delta(\cdot-y)I&\mbox{in }\mathbb{H}_{n},\\
\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\cdot G^{*}(\cdot,y)&=&0,&\\
G^{*}(\cdot,y)&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.13)

The Green function in (
2.12
) and its adjoint counterpart are also related by the symmetry property

G^{T}(y,\tilde{y})\\
=\,G^{*}(\tilde{y},y)\quad\mbox{ i.e. }\,G_{ij}(y,\tilde{y})=G_{ji}^{*}(\tilde{y},y).

(2.14)

Further, for
\varepsilon>0
we shall use Green’s functions
(G^{*,\varepsilon},\,\Pi^{*,\varepsilon})
associated to the coefficient
A^{*}(\cdot/\varepsilon)
in the domain
\mathbb{H}_{n}

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\cdot A^{*}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\,\nabla_{\tilde{x}}G^{*,\varepsilon}(\cdot,x)+\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\Pi^{*,\varepsilon}(\cdot,x)&=&\delta(\cdot-x)I&\mbox{in }\mathbb{H}_{n},\\
\nabla_{\tilde{x}}\cdot G^{*,\varepsilon}(\cdot,x)&=&0,\\
G^{*,\varepsilon}(\cdot,x)&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.15)

We shall also use Green’s functions associated to the homogenized operated
\mathcal{L}_{0}:=-\nabla\cdot A^{0}\>\nabla
in the half-space
\mathbb{H}_{n}
, precisely, the pair
(G^{0},\Pi^{0})=(G^{0}_{j},\Pi_{j})
solution to problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A^{0}\>\nabla G^{0}(\cdot,\tilde{y})+\nabla\Pi^{0}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&\delta(\cdot-\tilde{y})I&\mbox{in }\mathbb{H}_{n},\\
\nabla\cdot G^{0}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0,&\\
G^{0}(\cdot,\tilde{y})&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.16)

The existence and uniqueness of Green’s functions are established in
[
8
]
, Theorem 2.7, under the Assumption 2.5 therein that requires the weak solutions
(u,p)
of (
1.1
) to be locally Hölder continuous. Since
A
is assumed to be smooth the local Hölder continuity is automatically satisfied by
(u,p)
.
Moreover we have the following pointwise estimates on these Green functions
(G,\Pi)
, solution to problem (
2.12
) that shall be frequently used in our work.

Proposition 2.8
.

There exists a constant
C>0
depending on
d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
such that for all
y,\tilde{y}\in~\mathbb{H}_{n}
we have :

•

estimates on the velocity component
G(y,\tilde{y})

|G(y,\tilde{y})|\leq C\min\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-2}};\frac{y\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}};\frac{\tilde{y}\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}};\frac{(y\cdot n)(\tilde{y}\cdot n)}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}\right\}\!,

(2.17)

•

estimates on the first-order derivatives

|\nabla_{y}G(y,\tilde{y})|\leq C\min\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}};\frac{\tilde{y}\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}\right\}

(2.18)

and

|\nabla_{\tilde{y}}G(y,\tilde{y})|\leq C\min\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}};\frac{y\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}\right\}\!,

(2.19)

•

an estimate on the mixed derivative

|\nabla_{y}\!\nabla_{\tilde{y}}G(y,\tilde{y})|\leq\;\frac{C}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}},

(2.20)

•

estimates on the pressure component
\Pi(y,\tilde{y})
: for all
z\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
satisfying
|y-\tilde{y}|\leq|z-\tilde{y}|
we have

|\Pi(y,\tilde{y})-\Pi(z,\tilde{y})|\leq C\min\;\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}},\frac{\tilde{y}\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}\right\}

(2.21)

and

|\Pi(y,\tilde{y})|\leq C\min\;\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-1}},\frac{\tilde{y}\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}\right\},

(2.22)

•

an estimate on the derivative
\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\Pi
: for all
z\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
satisfying
|y-\tilde{y}|\leq|z-\tilde{y}|
we have

|\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\Pi(y,\tilde{y})-\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\Pi(z,\tilde{y})|\leq\;C\min\;\left\{\frac{1}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}};\frac{\tilde{y}\cdot n}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d+1}}\right\}

(2.23)

and

|\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\Pi(y,\tilde{y})|\leq\;\frac{C}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}.\\

(2.24)

A proof of Proposition
2.8
is given in Appendix
B
.

Remark 2.9
.

The Green functions
(G^{*},\Pi^{*})
associated to the adjoint operator
A^{*}
and defined in (
2.13
) satisfy the estimates in Proposition
2.8
with the interchanging of
y
and
\tilde{y}
. For instance, we have a ”star-version” of estimate (
2.20
)

|\nabla_{\tilde{y}}\nabla_{y}G^{*}(\tilde{y},y)|\leq\;\frac{C}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}},

(2.25)

and estimate (
2.24
)

|\nabla_{y}\Pi^{*}(\tilde{y},y)|\leq\;\frac{C}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d}}.

(2.26)

The homogenized Green functions
(G^{0},\Pi^{0})
satisfy estimates (
2.17
) through (
2.24
). Moreover, we have, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,

|\partial^{\lambda}_{y}G^{0}(y,\tilde{y})|\leq\;\frac{C}{|y-\tilde{y}|^{d-2+|\lambda|}}.

(2.27)

Lemma 2.10
.

The funtions
(G,\Pi)
and
(G^{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\varepsilon})
given respectively by problems
(
2.12
)
and
(
2.15
)
are related as follows

\displaystyle G^{\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-2}}G\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{\tilde{x}}{\varepsilon}\right)\quad\mbox{and }\quad\Pi^{\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-1}}\Pi\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{\tilde{x}}{\varepsilon}\right)\mbox{ for all }x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n}.

The functions
(G^{0},\Pi^{0})
given by
(
2.16
)
satisfy, for all
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
,

\displaystyle G^{0}(x,\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-2}}G^{0}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{\tilde{x}}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad\partial^{\lambda}_{\!x}G^{0}(x,\tilde{x})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,\partial^{\lambda}_{\!x}G^{0}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{\tilde{x}}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad\Pi^{0}(x,\tilde{x})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d-1}}\Pi^{0}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{\tilde{x}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Remark 2.11
.

The above estimates remain valid when the Green functions
(G,\Pi)
resp.
(G^{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\varepsilon})
are replaced with their adjoint counterparts
(G^{*},\Pi^{*})
resp.
(G^{*,\varepsilon},\Pi^{*,\varepsilon})
.

2.3.2.
Representation of the solution to the system (
1.1
)

When a solution
u
to problem (
1.1
) satisfies (
1.8
) then it is unique (proved in Appendix
C
) and it has the following integral representation

\displaystyle u(y)=\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left[-A^{*}(\tilde{y})\nabla_{\tilde{y}}G^{*}(\tilde{y},y)\cdot n\,g(\tilde{y})+\Pi^{*}(\tilde{y},y)g(\tilde{y})\cdot n\right]d\tilde{y}

that can be written into coordinates as follows

\displaystyle u_{i}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}P_{ij}(\tilde{y},y)g_{j}(\tilde{y})d\tilde{y}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\mbox{ where }\quad P_{ij}(y,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=-(A^{*})_{jk}^{\alpha\beta}(\tilde{y})\,\partial_{\tilde{y}_{\beta}}G_{ki}^{*}(\tilde{y},y)n_{\alpha}(\tilde{y})+\Pi_{i}^{*}(\tilde{y},y)n_{j}(\tilde{y})

(2.29)

\displaystyle=-n_{\alpha}A^{\beta\alpha}_{kj}(\tilde{y})\partial_{\tilde{y}_{\beta}}G_{ik}(y,\tilde{y})+\Pi^{*}_{i}(\tilde{y},y)n_{j}.

This function
P(y,\tilde{y})
is called Poisson’s kernel. In a condensed form we have

\displaystyle P(y,\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=-A^{*}(\tilde{y})\nabla_{\tilde{y}}G^{*}(\tilde{y},y)\cdot n(\tilde{y})+\Pi^{*}(\tilde{y},y)\otimes n.

3.
Homogenization in the half-space

The aim of this section is to establish a homogenization result which is going to be applied later in Section
4
. More precisely, we get an estimate of the difference
u^{\varepsilon}-u^{0}
between a heterogenous quantity and its associate homogenized counterpart. In the present context, the quantities
u^{\varepsilon},u^{0}
represent velocities respectively associated to some Stokes problems. More precisely, they are defined as follows.
Let
f
be a vector valued-function defined in
\overline{\mathbb{H}}_{n}
and consider the following system of equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla u^{\varepsilon}+\nabla p^{\varepsilon}&=&f&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{\varepsilon}&=&0,&\\
u^{\varepsilon}&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}.\end{array}\right.

(3.1)

The solution
(u^{\varepsilon}
,
p^{\varepsilon})
can be formally expanded as in (
2.4
), (
2.5
). We do so with the addition of boundary layer correctors so as to take into account the effects of the boundary :

\displaystyle u_{k}^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=u_{k}^{0}(x)+\varepsilon\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)+\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k}(x)+...

(3.2)

\displaystyle p^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=p^{0}(x)+\pi^{\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(x)+\varepsilon p^{\varepsilon}_{bl}(x)+...

(3.3)

where
(u^{0},p^{0})
is the solution of the homogenized system of equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\nabla\cdot A^{0}\>\nabla u^{0}+\nabla p^{0}&=&f&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
\nabla\cdot u^{0}&=&0,\\
u^{0}&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}\end{array}\right.

(3.4)

and
(u^{\varepsilon}_{bl},p^{\varepsilon}_{bl})
being the solution of the problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k})+\partial_{x_{i}}p^{\varepsilon}_{bl}&=&0&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
\partial_{x_{k}}u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k}&=&0,&\\
u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k}(x)&=&-\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{j}_{0}(x)&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}.\end{array}\right.

(3.5)

Now we intend to estimate the error terms in the approximations (
3.2
) and (
3.3
). To that end, we introduce
r^{\varepsilon}=(r^{\varepsilon}_{k})_{k}
and
q^{\varepsilon}
defined as follows

\displaystyle r_{k}^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle:=u_{k}^{\varepsilon}(x)-u_{k}^{0}(x)-\varepsilon\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(x)-\varepsilon u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k}(x),

(3.6)

\displaystyle q^{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle:=p^{\varepsilon}(x)-p^{0}(x)-\pi^{\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(x)-\varepsilon p^{\varepsilon}_{bl}(x).

(3.7)

Obtaining a bound on
r^{\varepsilon}
allows to get a control on the error
u^{\varepsilon}-u^{0}.

Moreover we assume that the source term
f
in (
3.1
) and (
3.4
) belongs to
C_{c}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}_{n})
. This property of the source term will turn out to be relevant in an application later in Section
4
. Indeed, the proof of the main result in that section is based on a duality argument.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\delta\in(0,d/2)
and
f\in C^{1}_{c}(B(0,1/2))
. There exists
C>0
depending on
d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
and
\delta

such that for all
\varepsilon>0
we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\|r^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}_{bl}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},

\displaystyle\|u^{\varepsilon}-u^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.8)

Remark 3.2
.

The pair
(r^{\varepsilon},q^{\varepsilon})
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcll}-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}r_{k}^{\varepsilon})+\partial_{x_{i}}q^{\varepsilon}&=&f_{i}^{\varepsilon},\quad i=1,...,d,&\mbox{in }\mathbb{H}_{n},\\
\partial_{x_{k}}r_{k}^{\varepsilon}&=&-\varepsilon\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{j}_{0}(x)&\mbox{in }{\mathbb{H}}_{n},\\
r^{\varepsilon}&=&0&\mbox{on }\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{n}.\end{array}\right.

(3.9)

where

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\varepsilon}=f_{i}

\displaystyle+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u_{k}^{0}\right)-\partial_{x_{i}}p_{0}

\displaystyle+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\!(\varepsilon\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{j}_{0})\right)-\partial_{x_{i}}\left(\pi^{\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{j}_{0}\right).

(3.10)

Here we have used the first equations in systems (
3.5
) and (
3.1
):

\displaystyle f_{i}=

\displaystyle-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{k}^{\varepsilon})+\partial_{x_{i}}p^{\varepsilon}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad 0=-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,k})+\partial_{x_{i}}p^{\varepsilon}_{bl}.

The quantity
f^{\varepsilon}
, given in the expression (
3.10
), apparently contains terms of order zero in
\varepsilon
. Yet
we would like to factorize it by
\varepsilon
. To that end, we make some transformation of
f^{\varepsilon}
given in (
3.10
).

Lemma 3.3
.

The source term
f^{\varepsilon}
in
(
3.9
)
can be factorized in the following way

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\left[\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}^{\gamma}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{i}}\{h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{i}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{i}^{\alpha}(x)\}\right]

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\left[(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}(x)+(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{i}(x)\right]

(3.11)

where we have set

\displaystyle(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}^{\gamma}(x):=

\displaystyle\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),\quad h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(x):=q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),

\displaystyle(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{i}(x):=

\displaystyle q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),

(3.12)

\displaystyle(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{i}^{\alpha}(x):=

\displaystyle A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)

and

\displaystyle(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}(x):=\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}\partial_{x_{\gamma}}u_{j}^{0}(x),\qquad(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{i}(x):=2q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)

(3.13)

with the functions
\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij},\,q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}
being given by the following Lemma.

We now state the following classical lemma

Lemma 3.4
.

(
[
16
]
, Lemma 2.2.)

Let us set

b^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y):=A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)+A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)-(A^{0})^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}.

Then
for fixed
\alpha,\beta,j\in\{1,...,d\}
there exists
(\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij},q^{\alpha\beta}_{j})\in H^{1}(Y)^{d}\times H^{1}(Y)
such that

b^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j},\qquad\pi^{\beta}_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}.

and

\displaystyle\|\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}+\|q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(Y)}\leq C,

where
C>0
depends only on
d,\mu
and
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
.

Proof of
Lemma
3.3
.

By the first equation in (
2.2
)
f_{i}^{\varepsilon}
can be rewritten

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\varepsilon}=f_{i}^{0}+f_{i}^{1}

(3.14)

with

\displaystyle f_{i}^{0}:=

\displaystyle f_{i}-\partial_{x_{i}}p^{0}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}(y)\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u_{k}^{0}\right)+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}\right)-\pi^{\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}

and

\displaystyle f_{i}^{1}:

\displaystyle=\partial_{y_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)\right)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}+\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}\right).

Using the homogenized equation,
-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{0})^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u_{j}^{0}+\partial_{x_{i}}p^{0}=f_{i},\
we rewrite
f^{0}
as

\displaystyle f_{i}^{0}=

\displaystyle-\partial_{x_{\alpha}}(A^{0})^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u_{j}^{0}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\left(A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\beta}}\!u_{j}^{0}\right)+[\partial_{x_{\alpha}}A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}]-\pi^{\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-(A^{0})^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}+A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)+A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(y)\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(y)\right)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}-\pi^{\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}.

Now we use Lemma
3.4
to obtain

\displaystyle f_{i}^{0}=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)+\partial_{y_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\right)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}-\partial_{y_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}+\partial_{y_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}-\partial_{y_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}.

The terms in the right hand side of the above equation can be factorized as follows. From the identity

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\!\left(\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}\!(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\!\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)\right)\!=

\displaystyle\>(1/\varepsilon)[\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\!\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)+[\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\!\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)

we deduce

\displaystyle[\partial_{y_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\;\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\{\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon).

Similarly we have

\displaystyle[\partial_{y_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\partial_{x_{i}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)

and

\displaystyle[\partial_{y_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}](x,x/\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon).

Thus, one obtains a factorization of
f^{0}_{i}
in
\varepsilon

\displaystyle f_{i}^{0}(x)=

\displaystyle\;\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\{\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\partial_{x_{i}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{i}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}-\varepsilon[\partial_{x_{\alpha}}q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}](x,x/\varepsilon).

Consequently we get

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\left(\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\{\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}+\partial_{x_{i}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\}\right)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\left(\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}\partial_{x_{\gamma}}u_{j}^{0}+2q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}\right)(x,x/\varepsilon).

Now, by setting, on the one hand

\displaystyle(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}^{\gamma}(x):=

\displaystyle\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),\qquad h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(x):=q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),

\displaystyle(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{i}(x):=

\displaystyle q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x),\,\quad(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{i}^{\alpha}(x):=A^{\alpha\gamma}_{ik}(x/\varepsilon)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(x/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)

and on the other hand,

\displaystyle(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}(x):=\phi^{\gamma\alpha\beta}_{ij}(y)\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}\partial_{x_{\gamma}}u_{j}^{0}(x),\qquad(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{i}(x):=2q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}(y)\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(x)

one gets

\displaystyle f_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)=

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon\left[\partial_{x_{\gamma}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}^{\gamma}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{i}}\{h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{i}(x)\}+\partial_{x_{\alpha}}\{(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{i}^{\alpha}(x)\}\right]

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\left[(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{i}(x)+(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{i}(x)\right].\qquad\qquad\qed

Lemma 3.5
.

Assume that the source term
f
in
(
3.1
)
and
(
3.4
)
satisfies

\Supp(f)\subset B(0,1/2)
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
depending on
d,\mu,\delta
and
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}

such that, for
x\in\mathbb{H}_{n}
and
|x|\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle|h^{\varepsilon}_{j}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{d}}\,\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},\quad j=1,2,3,4,

(3.15)

\displaystyle|g^{\varepsilon}_{j}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{d+1}}\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},\quad j=1,2.

(3.16)

and

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\|_{L^{d+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

(3.17)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

(3.18)

Proof.

Recall that
u^{0}
is the solution to problem (
3.4
) and as such it can be represented through the Green kernel as follows

u^{0}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}G^{0}(x,\tilde{x})f(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}.

Hence, for a multi-index
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{d},
we have

\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\partial^{\lambda}_{x}G^{0}(x,\tilde{x})f(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}.

which implies

\displaystyle|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}G^{0}(x,\tilde{x})||f(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}.

Then estimate (
2.27
) implies

\displaystyle|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,|f(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B(0,1/2)}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,|f(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}.

Since for
|x|\geq 1
and
\tilde{x}\in B(0,1/2)
we have
|x-\tilde{x}|\geq|x|-1/2
the inequality right above leads to

\displaystyle|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B(0,1/2)}\frac{C}{\left(|x|-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,|f(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}.

Hölder inequality then yields

\displaystyle|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(|x|-\frac{1}{2})^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,\left(\int_{B(0,1/2)}1d\tilde{x}\right)^{1/p^{\prime}}\left(\int_{B(0,1/2)}|f(\tilde{x})|^{p}d\tilde{x}\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(|x|-\frac{1}{2})^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,C(d,p)\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

with
p=d/2+\delta
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
.
Then by noticing that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(|x|-\frac{1}{2})^{d-2+|\lambda|}}=\frac{1}{|x|^{d-2+|\lambda|}\left(1-\frac{1}{2|x|}\right)^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\qquad\mbox{ and }\quad 1-\frac{1}{2|x|}\geq\frac{1}{2}

one obtains

\displaystyle|\partial^{\lambda}_{x}u^{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{d-2+|\lambda|}}\,\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},\quad\mbox{ for all }|x|\geq 1.

The constant
C
in the above lines depends on
d,\mu,\delta
and
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
.
This last estimate with
|\lambda|=2,3
together with the boundedness of the functions
\,A^{\alpha\beta}_{ij},\chi^{\beta}_{kj},\,q^{\alpha\beta}_{j}
given in Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
3.4
leads to

\displaystyle|h^{\varepsilon}_{j}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{d}}\,\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},\quad j=1,2,3,4

and to

\displaystyle|g^{\varepsilon}_{j}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{d+1}}\|f\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})},\quad j=1,2.

where
C
depends on
d,\mu,\delta
and
[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
.
∎

Now we continue the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Recall that the aim is to establish a bound on
r^{\varepsilon}
solution to problem (
3.9
).
Let us start with an integral representation of
r^{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}f_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}-\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\left(-\varepsilon\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(\tilde{x})\right)d\tilde{x}.

We replace
f^{\varepsilon}_{i}
in the first integral by its expression given in (
3.11
)

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)=

\displaystyle\quad\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left[\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\gamma}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{k}^{\gamma}(\tilde{x})\}+\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\{h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\tilde{x})\}+\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}\{(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{k}(\tilde{x})\}+\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}\{(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{k}^{\alpha}(\tilde{x})\}\right]G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left[(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{k}(\tilde{x})+(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{k}(\tilde{x})\right]G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(\tilde{x})\,d\tilde{x}.

Then one performs integration by parts for some of the above terms

\displaystyle r_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)=

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left[(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{k}^{\alpha}(\tilde{x})+(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{k}(\tilde{x})+(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{k}^{\alpha}(\tilde{x})\right]\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}h^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\tilde{x})\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left[(g^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{k}(\tilde{x})+(g^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{k}(\tilde{x})\right]G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\,d\tilde{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(\tilde{x})\,d\tilde{x}

which implies

\displaystyle|r_{i}^{\varepsilon}(x)|\leq\,\varepsilon\!\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}

\displaystyle\left|(h^{\varepsilon})^{\alpha}_{k}(\tilde{x})\right|\left|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x}+\varepsilon\!\int_{\mathbb{H}}\left|(h_{2}^{\varepsilon})(\tilde{x})\right|\left|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}|g_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\left|G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left|\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\right|\,\left|\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(\tilde{x})\right|\,d\tilde{x}

(3.19)

with

\displaystyle|(h^{\varepsilon})^{\alpha}_{k}|:=

\displaystyle|(h^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{k}^{\alpha}|+|(h^{\varepsilon}_{3})^{\alpha}_{k}|+|(h_{4}^{\varepsilon})_{k}^{\alpha}|,

(3.20)

\displaystyle|g^{\varepsilon}|:=

\displaystyle|g^{\varepsilon}_{1}|+|g^{\varepsilon}_{2}|.

Step 1 : an estimate of
r^{\varepsilon}
in the
L^{\infty}
norm

We rewrite the bound (
3.19
) as follows:

\displaystyle|r^{\varepsilon}(x)|\leq

\displaystyle\>\varepsilon\,(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4})

(3.21)

with

\displaystyle I_{1}:=\!\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}|(h^{\varepsilon})^{\alpha}_{k}(\tilde{x})|\left|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x},

\displaystyle I_{2}:=\!\int_{\mathbb{H}}|(h_{2}^{\varepsilon})(\tilde{x})|\left|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x},

\displaystyle I_{3}:=\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}|g_{k}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\left|G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\right|d\tilde{x},

\displaystyle I_{4}:=\int_{\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left|\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\right|\left|\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u^{0}_{j}(\tilde{x})\right|\,d\tilde{x}.

Now we seek to bound the quantities
I_{k}
,
k=1,2,3,4.

We extend
(h^{\varepsilon})^{\alpha}_{k},\,(h_{2}^{\varepsilon}),\,g^{\varepsilon},\,u^{0}
to the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by setting

\displaystyle(h^{\varepsilon})^{\alpha}_{k}(\tilde{x})=(h_{2}^{\varepsilon})(\tilde{x})=g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})=u^{0}(\tilde{x})=0\quad\mbox{for all }\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\overline{\mathbb{H}}_{n}.

Step 1-a : Control of
I_{1}

The bound (
2.19
) on Green’s function
\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})
gives

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\,\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=:\,I_{1,1}+I_{1,2}

where the constant
C
depends on
d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
.
Let us first control
I_{1,1}
. We take
p,p^{\prime}\in(1,+\infty)
satisfying
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and
p<\frac{d}{d-1}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{|\tilde{x}|<1}\,\left(\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\right)^{\!\!{}^{p}}\!d\tilde{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{|\tilde{x}|<1}\,\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-1)p}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,C(d)\int_{0}^{1}\,\frac{r^{d-1}}{r^{(d-1)p}}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{C(d)}{d-(d-1)p}=C(d,p)<\infty.

So we have

\tilde{x}\mapsto\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The condition
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
combined with
p<\frac{d}{d-1}
implies
p^{\prime}>d
i.e.
p^{\prime}=d+\delta
for some
\delta>0
.
From the definitions (
3.12
) and (
3.20
) and from Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
3.4
we derive the bound

\displaystyle|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|

\displaystyle\leq C([A]_{C^{0,\eta}},d,\mu)|\nabla^{2}u^{0}(\tilde{x})|,\quad\mbox{ for all }\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n},

(3.22)

which implies

\displaystyle\|h^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{d+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\|h^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{d+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C([A]_{C^{0,\eta}},d,\mu)\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\|_{L^{d+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

Moreover, Sobolev’s embedding theorem yields

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\|_{L^{d+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\delta)\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

This Sobolev’s embedding holds for
\delta\in(0,d/2)
.
By the regularity theory of the Stokes problem (see
[
10
]
, Theorem IV.3.2), we get

\displaystyle\|u^{0}\|_{W^{3,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\|h^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C([A]_{C^{0,\eta}},d,\mu,\delta)\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

Finally we apply Hölder’s inequality to
I_{1,1}

\displaystyle I_{1,1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{|\cdot-x|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(x,1)}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\|h^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C([A]_{C^{0,\eta}},d,\mu,\delta)\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

We now control the term
I_{1,2}
:

\displaystyle I_{1,2}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

(3.23)

If
q=d/2+\delta
and
q^{\prime}
is defined by
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
then we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}\leq C\left\|\frac{1}{|\cdot|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\right\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\left\||h^{\varepsilon}|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}\right\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

because with such a
q^{\prime}
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{{B(0,1)^{c}}}\left|\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\right|^{q^{\prime}}d\tilde{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{|\tilde{x}|>1}\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-1)q^{\prime}}}d\tilde{x}<\infty.

So by recalling the fact that

\displaystyle\left\|h^{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\leq C\left\|\nabla^{2}u^{0}\right\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\leq\;C\left\|f\right\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\leq\;C\left\|f\right\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})\,d\tilde{x}\leq

\displaystyle\;C\left\|f\right\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

(3.24)

with
C=C(d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)
.

The term

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

is estimated as follows. On the one hand, for
p>\frac{d}{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-1)p}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{|\tilde{x}|>1}\,\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-1)p}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=C(d,p)<\infty,

and on the other hand, by (
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|^{p^{\prime}}_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\int_{|\tilde{x}|>1}\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{dp^{\prime}}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|^{p^{\prime}}_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

whenever the inequality
p^{\prime}>1
holds. Which is indeed te case when
p^{\prime}
is given by
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. We then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|h^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}\leq C(d,\mu,\delta,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})\left\|f\right\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.25)

Putting together (
3.23
), (
3.24
) and (
3.25
) we get

\displaystyle I_{1,2}\leq C(d,\mu,\delta,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})\left\|f\right\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.26)

We thus have obtained

\displaystyle I_{1}=I_{1,1}+I_{1,2}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

where
C=C(d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)>0
and
\delta\in(0,d/2)
.

Step 1-b : Control of the term
I_{2}

The reasoning that has lead to the above bound of
I_{1}
remains valid when
I_{1}
is replaced with the integral
I_{2}
. Therefore, we also have

\displaystyle I_{2}\leq C(d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)\,\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

Step 1-c : Control of the term
I_{3}

By the estimate (
2.17
) we have

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=I_{3,1}+I_{3,2}.

First, we bound the term
I_{3,1}
as follows. Let
p,p^{\prime}\in(1,+\infty)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
and
p<\frac{d}{d-2}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B(0,1)}\left|\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\right|^{p}d\tilde{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B(0,1)}\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-2)p}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C(d,p)<\infty.

Under the above condition on
p
the exponent
p^{\prime}
satisfies
p^{\prime}>d/2
i.e.
p^{\prime}=d/2+\delta
for some
\delta>0
. From the definitions of
g^{\varepsilon}_{j}
in (
3.13
) we get

\displaystyle|g^{\varepsilon}_{j}(\tilde{x})|

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mu)|\nabla^{3}u^{0}(\tilde{x})|,\quad\mbox{ for all }\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n},

which implies

\displaystyle\|g^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mu)\|\nabla^{3}u^{0}\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mu,\delta)\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.27)

Now we apply Hausdorff-Young inequality to obtain

\displaystyle I_{3,1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{|\cdot|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\!\!\!\|g^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.28)

with
C=C(d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)
.

We now control the integral

\displaystyle I_{3,2}=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}.

(3.29)

One handles the term

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

through an application of Hausdorff-Young inequality.
To do so, we set
q=d/2+\delta
and define
q^{\prime}
by
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=1
.
Then we have
q^{\prime}>\frac{d}{d-2}
and consequently we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\left(\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\right)^{\!\!q^{\prime}}\!\!\!\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}=

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,1)^{c}}\left|\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\right|^{q^{\prime}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle C(d,\delta)<\infty.

The Hausdorff-Young inequality yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}\leq C(d,\mu,\delta,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})\|g^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and subsequently estimate (
3.27
) implies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mu,\delta,,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}.

(3.30)

The term

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

is estimated as follows. On the one hand, for
p>\frac{d}{d-2}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-2)p}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{|\tilde{x}|>1}\,\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d-2)p}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle=C(d,p)<\infty,

and on the other hand, by (
3.16
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|^{p^{\prime}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|^{p^{\prime}}_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}\int_{|\tilde{x}|>1}\frac{1}{|\tilde{x}|^{(d+1)p^{\prime}}}d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|f\right\|^{p^{\prime}}_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

whenever
p^{\prime}>\frac{d}{d+1}
, which is indeed the case when
p^{\prime}
is given by
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. We then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-2}}\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(x-\tilde{x})|g^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})|\mathbf{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}\leq C(d,\mu,\delta,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})\left\|f\right\|_{L^{d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

(3.31)

where
C=C(d,\mu,\delta,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}})
.
Putting together (
3.29
), (
3.30
) and (
3.31
) we obtain

\displaystyle I_{3}=I_{3,1}+I_{3,2}\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

with
C=C\left(A,d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta\right)
.

Step 1-d : Control of
I_{4}

The method carried out to bound integral
I_{1}
still applies because on the one hand we have the pointwise estimate
(
2.22
)
for
\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}
similar to that of
\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\alpha}}G_{ki}^{*,\varepsilon}
: for some
C
depending on
d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}}
one has

\displaystyle|\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x-\tilde{x}|^{d-1}},\quad\mbox{ for all }x,\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n},

and on the other hand we have the bound

\displaystyle\left|\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\partial_{\tilde{x}_{k}}\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(\tilde{x})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\mu)|\nabla^{2}u^{0}(\tilde{x})|,\mbox{ for all }\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{H}_{n}.

Thus one has the estimate

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{W^{1,\,d/2+\delta}(\mathbb{H}_{n})}

where
C=C(d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)>0
with
\delta\in(0,d/2)
.

Conclusion.
The inequality (
3.21
) together with the preceding bounds on
I_{k}
,
k=1,2,3,4,
yields

\displaystyle|r^{\varepsilon}(x)|\leq\,C\varepsilon\|f\|_{W^{1,d/2+\delta}},\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathbb{H}_{n},

(3.32)

where
C=C(A,d,\mu,[A]_{C^{0,\eta}},\delta)>0
with
\delta\in(0,d/2)
.

Step 2 : an estimate of
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}
in the
L^{\infty}
-norm

Let us remind that the function
u_{bl}^{\varepsilon}
is the solution to problem (
3.5
). Therefore it has the following representation

\displaystyle u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,i}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}-P_{ik}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\,\left(\chi(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\cdot\nabla u^{0}(\tilde{x})\right)_{k}d\tilde{x},\qquad\mbox{for }x\in\mathbb{H}_{n},

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}-P_{ik}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})\,\chi^{\beta}_{kj}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\,\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(\tilde{x})d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\mbox{ where }\quad P_{ik}^{*,\varepsilon}(x,\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=-(A^{*})_{kr}^{\alpha\gamma}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\,\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\gamma}}G_{ri}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)n_{\alpha}(\tilde{x})+\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)n_{k}(\tilde{x}).

Then we get, using the boundedness of
\chi
and on
A
, given in Lemma
2.3
,

\displaystyle|u^{\varepsilon}_{bl,i}(x)|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left|(A^{*})_{kr}^{\gamma\beta}(\tilde{x}/\varepsilon)\,\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\gamma}}G_{ri}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)n_{\alpha}(\tilde{x})\right|\,|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left|\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)n_{k}(\tilde{x})\right|\,|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,C\int_{\partial\mathbb{H}_{n}}\left(\left|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}G_{ri}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\right|+\left|\Pi_{i}^{*,\varepsilon}(\tilde{x},x)\right|\right)|\partial_{\tilde{x}_{\beta}}u_{j}^{0}(\tilde{x})|d\tilde{x}

where
C
depends on
A
.
Next we apply the star-version of estimates (
2.19
) and (
